# Parallels index: the Mesopotamian creation stories and the Bible

This is the corpus's main product for the commentary (`corpus.md`; guidelines §7.3).
It merges the parallels indexes of the projects into one table ordered by Bible verse: Genesis 1–11 first (by chapter), then the rest of Genesis, Exodus–Deuteronomy, and the rest of the Bible.
Each row is filed under its first Genesis verse (otherwise its first verse). The column "Text, line" gives a siglum and the line: **EE** = Enūma eliš (project 1; tablet and line in `1-אנומה-אליש/translation.rtl.md`, apparatus in `1-אנומה-אליש/annotated-<tablet>.rtl.md`); project 2 (`2-טקסטי-בריאה-ועדן/`, apparatus in `annotated-<N>-<Name>.rtl.md`): **Adapa** (MB = Amarna, SB = Nineveh), **E&Nḫ** = Enki and Ninḫursaĝa, **E&Nm** = Enki and Ninmaḫ, **Hoe** = the Song of the Hoe, **G&S** = Grain and Sheep, **Eridu** = the bilingual "Creation of the World by Marduk" (BM 93014), **KAR 4**. Each project's own indexes are in its `project.md`.
The Hebrew rendering is ours, as it stands in the translation. Bible quotations are vocalized and checked with `tools/check_bible_quotes.py`.
**Generated** from the indexes in `1-אנומה-אליש/project.md`, `2-טקסטי-בריאה-ועדן/project.md` (2026-10-16/17), `3-הציפור-והדג/project.md` and `4-אנקי-וסדר-העולם/project.md` (2026-10-19; sigla **B&F**, **EWO**); the key findings below were written by the corpus coordinator. When a tablet index changes, regenerate the table with `python3 -I 1-אנומה-אליש/build_parallels_index.py` (it keeps everything above the heading of the table).
Atra-ḫasīs I, the creation of man in the flood corpus, is cross-referenced at the end.

## Key findings for the commentary (Genesis 1 first)

The commentary asks of each word in Genesis 1: *why exactly this word?* These are the places where Enūma eliš answers, or sharpens, that question.

1. **`תְהוֹם` (1:2) and Tiāmat.** *tiāmtu* is the cognate of `תְהוֹם`, not its source (Hebrew has no feminine *-at* and keeps the guttural). In the poem she is a mother who bears, mingles her waters with Apsû (I 3–5), plots, fights and is killed; in Genesis the word is inert and has no article, almost a name. The translation never renders her `תְהוֹם`. (Gen 1, row 1:2.)
2. **There is no *raqīʿu*.** Marduk's sky (IV 137–140) is half of Tiāmat's corpse set up as a roof (*ṣullulu*, rendered `קֵרָה`, the verb of `הַמְקָרֶה בַמַּיִם עֲלִיּוֹתָיו`, תהלים קד:ג), a stretched skin and posted guards, with orders "not to let her waters out". The shared function is a barrier holding back the upper waters (1:6–8); what Genesis adds with `רקע` (beaten-out sheet metal) is a made surface, and what it removes is the corpse, the skin, the guards and the enemy. Division is by *ḫepû* "split" and *zâzu* "divide": Akkadian has no counterpart of `בדל`, and the translation never uses `הִבְדִּיל`. Foster's English "firmament" (IV 141, V 121, VII 135) is a false echo of the Vulgate: the Akkadian words are plain poetic words for heaven or place. (1:6–8.)
3. **The tanninim (1:21) – the unit the commentary is editing now.** Tiāmat's eleven creatures – serpents, "great dragons" (*ušumgallū*, Sumerian "great serpent", the closest match to `הַתַּנִּינִם הַגְּדֹלִים`), the venomous *bašmu* (cognate of `פֶּתֶן`) – are "brought into being" (*uštabši*, I 146) by the chaos mother as weapons against the gods; they are captured and trampled (IV 115–118); their images (*ṣalmu*) are set at the gate of the Apsû as "a sign (*ittu*) never to be forgotten" (V 73–76). The same causative of "to be" brings man into being (VI 5): **the two objects of `בָּרָא` after 1:1 in Genesis (the tanninim, 1:21; man, 1:27) are exactly the two things that the Akkadian brings into being with *šubšû***. Genesis inverts the monsters' role: created, seen as good and blessed (1:21–22), not fought; the combat survives only outside Genesis (תהלים עד:יג; ישעיהו נא:ט; איוב כו:יב–יג). Deut 32:33 brings together venom, `תַּנִּינִם`, `פְּתָנִים` and `אַכְזָר`, the very vocabulary of I 136, 141, 143. (1:21–22.)
4. **Creation by word, and `וַיְהִי כֵן`.** IV 22 *abātu u banû qibi liktūnā* "to destroy and to build – command, and let them be firm": *kânu* is the root of `כֵּן`. IV 25–26: he spoke and the constellation was destroyed, spoke again and it was rebuilt. The divergence: the power of the word is conferred on Marduk by the assembly, on his condition (II 156–162), and it destroys as well as builds. (1:3, 7.)
5. **Naming is existence.** The poem opens with heaven and earth not yet named (I 1–2: *nabû*, `קָרָא`; *šuma zakāru*, `הִזְכִּיר שֵׁם`), and the gods not yet "called by name" (I 8); Ea names his dwelling Apsû (I 76); man is created with "let 'man' be his name" (VI 6; ↔ 5:2); the poem ends with fifty names (VI 121 – VII 144). `קָרָא` is reserved in the translation for *nabû* so that the reader can see where the naming verb stands. (1:5, 8, 10; 2:19–20; 5:2.)
6. **The luminaries.** The moon god's name *Nannaru* means "luminary": the epithet that Genesis gives to an unnamed "lesser light" (1:16) is in Akkadian a god's name (V 12, rendered `נַנָּרוּ`, never `מָאוֹר`). Marduk "marks" (*uddû*) the year and the days (V 3, 13; ↔ 1:14), sets the stars as the gods' stations (V 1–2), and the sun (*šamšu*) is named (V 19), as Genesis avoids. The "seventh day" (V 17) and *šapattu* (V 18) are days of the lunar month, a half-disk and a full moon. (1:14–18; 2:2–3.)
7. **Vegetation brought forth.** VII 2 *mušēṣû urqīti* "who brings forth the green" has two cognates at once (Š of *aṣû* = `הוֹצִיא`, *urqītu* = `יֶרֶק`) ↔ 1:12 and 1:30; VII 57, 69 *dešû* D is the exact cognate of `תַּדְשֵׁא … דֶּשֶׁא` (1:11); VII 61 *edû* = `אֵד` (2:6). In Genesis the earth brings forth; in the poem a god does. (1:11–12, 29–30; 2:6.)
8. **Man.** Man (*lullû*, "'man' (*amēlu*) shall be his name") is made from the blood of the guilty god Qingu, with bone (*eṣemtu* = `עֶצֶם`, VI 5), to bear the gods' toil so that they may rest (VI 8, 33–36). Genesis: image and dominion (1:26–28), not substitute labour; God's rest replaces nobody. *šipru* "work" (VI 37, 57, 67), like `מְלָאכָה` (2:2–3), comes from a verb "to send". (1:26–28; 2:2–3, 7, 15, 23.)
9. **Rest.** The poem's rest words are *nâḫu* (cognate of `וַיָּנַח`, שמות כ:י) and *pašāḫu* (rendered `נִרְגַּע`); Akkadian has no verb *šabātu*. Ea rests (*inūḫ*) in his shrine after killing Apsû (I 75); Marduk rests before creating (IV 135), the reverse of Genesis's order; the gods rest because man bears their work (VI 8, 34, 130). II 100 *libbuš lippuš* "may her heart be refreshed" has the cognate of `וַיִּנָּפַשׁ` (שמות לא:יז), and II 59 pairs the two rest nouns as ישעיהו כח:יב does. (2:1–3.)
10. **Likeness.** Anu begets Nudimmud as his *tamšīlu* "likeness" (I 16) ↔ `וַיּוֹלֶד בִּדְמוּתוֹ כְּצַלְמוֹ` (5:3); the Ešgalla is the "likeness" of the Apsû (IV 144), and "the likeness of what he made in heaven, let him make on earth" (VI 112) ↔ the Tabernacle's `תַּבְנִית` (שמות כה:ט, מ); *ṣalmu* = `צֶלֶם` is used for the monsters' images (V 75), not for man. (1:26–27; 5:3.)

Beyond Gen 1: the four winds that stir up Tiāmat (I 105–110) ↔ Daniel 7:2–3 and, as divergence, 1:2 (*dalāḫu* is the cognate of `וַתִּדְלַח מַיִם`, of the `תַּנִּים` in יחזקאל לב:ב); Euphrates and Tigris from Tiāmat's eyes (V 55) ↔ 2:14; mountains on her breasts after the deep waters (V 54, 57) ↔ 49:25; the bricks of Babylon made for a year (*labānu* = `לבן`, VI 60) and Esagil's "raised head" (VI 62) ↔ 11:3–4; the bow set in heaven as a star after the war with the waters (VI 82–91) ↔ 9:13; the song of Marduk ending in kingship (VII 161–162) ↔ Exodus 15.

## Key findings for the commentary: project 2 (Genesis 2–4, Exodus 4, Leviticus 21)

Written by the corpus coordinator from the seven texts' indexes (2026-10-17). Proposals of ours, not found in the literature available to us, are marked as such in the rows.

1. **The food of life refused (Adapa).** "To him he gave wisdom; eternal life he did not give him" (SB a+4) is the closest Mesopotamian statement of the two gifts of 2:9 / 3:22 (but *nēmequ* is "wisdom", not `דַּעַת`). MB 68′ *akal balāṭi liqâniššum-ma līkul* has the two cognate verbs of 3:22 in the same order (`וְלָקַח … וְאָכַל`): Genesis fears the act, Anu orders it. Ea's ban (MB 33′–34′, *lā* + present = `לֹא תֹאכַל`) proves misleading like `מוֹת תָּמוּת` / `לֹא מוֹת תְּמֻתוּן`, with the outcome reversed (Adapa obeys and loses life). MB 74′ "why did you not eat?" against 3:11 "did you eat?"; SB e+4 Anu laughs at what Ea did (the trickster ↔ `הַנָּחָשׁ הִשִּׁיאַנִי`, 3:13); MB 77′ "take him and return him to his ground" (*leqû*, *târu* = `שׁוּב`) ↔ 3:19, 3:23 (partly restored). The garment and oil (MB 35′–36′, 71′–72′: *labāšu* = `לָבַשׁ`) differ from 3:21: Adapa dresses himself, the oil ends mourning. Outside Genesis: Ezek 28:12–16.
2. **Dilmun is not Eden, and its "curse" is an oath (Enki and Ninḫursaĝa).** Dilmun's purity is told by plain negations (no "not yet": ETCSL's translation imports `טֶרֶם`); 13–14 have Isa 65:25's animals and 20–21 Isa 33:24's formula. Water rises from the earth (*ed₃* = `עָלָה`, 43–44, 54–55 ↔ 2:6), and the irrigation verb (`הִשְׁקָה`) is used of Enki's semen (72). The eight plants are grown by the goddess (*mu₂* = `הִצְמִיחָה` ↔ 2:9), handed to Enki by his vizier and eaten one by one (190–219 ↔ 3:6), with **no prohibition** ([absent] for 2:17); Enki "decides their fate" and "knows their heart" (*zu* = `יָדַע` ↔ 3:7). Ninḫursaĝa's words (220–221) are *nam-erim₂ kud*, the ordinary idiom for swearing an oath, not a curse ("by the eye of life, until he dies I shall not look at him"; `עֵין הַחַיִּים` / `עֵץ הַחַיִּים` is a Hebrew-only resemblance). **Nin-ti (268–269, 279):** TI is "rib" and "life" in Sumerian; Hebrew `צֵלָע` has no such pun, so the famous derivation of `אֵם כָּל חָי` needs an intermediary we do not have; what is shared is the technique (a name explained by its origin, as `אִשָּׁה` / `אִישׁ`, 2:23). The healing scene inverts 2:21–22: a goddess bears from a male body.
3. **Ex 4:11 and Lev 21 (Enki and Ninmaḫ) – our proposal.** Ninmaḫ, drunk, fashions six defective humans; Enki "decrees a fate" and gives each "bread" and a place. The order blind man (62) – crippled feet matches Lev 21:18 `עִוֵּר אוֹ פִסֵּחַ`; the weak hands (59) `שֶׁבֶר יָד`, the sexless one (75) `מְרוֹחַ אָשֶׁךְ` / Deut 23:2. Every one receives bread, as the blemished priest still eats `לֶחֶם אֱלֹהָיו` (Lev 21:22); but where Leviticus says `לֹא יִקְרַב`, three of them are set "before the king" (61, 65, 78; `וַיַּעֲמֹד לְפָנָיו`, 1 Sam 16:21) – the central inversion. Umul (83–101) "cannot put bread in his mouth" (90, *ĝar* = `שָׂם`: both words of `מִי שָׂם פֶּה לָאָדָם`) and "does not know how to speak" (96 ↔ `לֹא אִישׁ דְּבָרִים אָנֹכִי`, Ex 4:10). The theological divergence: two gods, one making the defect and one assigning the place; in Ex 4:11 one God makes seeing and blind. The sexless courtier ↔ Isa 56:3–5; the woman who cannot bear "built" into the women's house (74 ↔ `אִבָּנֶה מִמֶּנָּה`, Gen 16:2). Gen 2: clay (*im* = `טִיט`) "above the Abzu" nipped off (*kir₃* = `קרץ`, Job 33:6), the basket of the gods' toil laid on man (30 ↔ 2:15), and the god asleep who must be woken (14 ↔ 2:21, divergence).
4. **Man from the ground, and the hoe (Song of the Hoe).** Enlil separates heaven from earth (4–5, *bad*, no waters, no firmament) "so that the seed of the Land may come out of the earth" (3; three manuscripts "go up from the earth", the phrase of 2:6): Gen 1:6–8 and 1:12 in one act. At Uzuea "heads" sprout (6), the hoe is driven in, the head of mankind is set in the brick mould (19, *ĝar* = `שָׂם` ↔ `וַיָּשֶׂם`, 2:8), and the people "break through the earth" toward Enlil (20, *ki dar*, the verb of sprouting): man rises by himself from the earth, moulded like a brick, not shaped by a potter (↔ 2:7, 3:19, 3:23 – the `אָדָם`/`אֲדָמָה` pun is Hebrew only; *ki* is always `הָאָרֶץ`). The god names them (29 ↔ 5:2); they are made to feed the gods (25, 31 ↔ 1:29 reversed); the same hoe buries (74–75 ↔ 3:19). Work is the hoe's gift, not a curse (97–106 ↔ 3:17–18).
5. **The first humans without bread or clothes (Grain and Sheep).** "The people of those days did not know eating bread, did not know wearing clothes; the Land went about with bare limbs; like sheep they ate grass with their mouths" (20–25) – Gen 3:18–19 and 2:25 / 3:7, 3:21 reversed as a culture history; every negation of the prologue is plain *nu-*, so `טֶרֶם` (2:5) is a divergence. Line 2 opens "on the day when" (↔ `בְּיוֹם עֲשׂוֹת`, 2:4b). On ETCSL's own lemma, grain and sheep are not "created" but "dwell" with the gods on the Holy Mound (27, 39) and are brought down to men (40–42): the goods come down, man is not sent out (↔ 3:23). The debate is Gen 4 inverted: the farmer attacks with a weapon (125), envy runs from shepherd to farmer (123), and the gods' verdict is for **grain** (184, 193) – the reverse of 4:4–5 (in Dumuzid and Enkimdu the shepherd wins). `אִכָּר` and `נֹקֵד` are Mesopotamian loanwords.
6. **The "not yet" chain and the waters (Founding of Eridu, BM 93014).** Obv. 1–9 are *ul* + stative: "a holy house had not been made, a reed had not come forth, a tree had not been built, a brick had not been laid …" – a list of culture before nature, closer to Prov 8:26 `עַד-לֹא עָשָׂה` than to 2:5. "All the lands were sea" (10) ↔ 1:2, 1:9; Marduk ties a reed raft "on the face of the waters" (17, *ina pān mê* = `עַל פְּנֵי הַמַּיִם`, cognate), makes dust and pours it beside it (18, *eperu* = `עָפָר`), fills a terrace and sets the dry land (31–32) – dry land made by labour against the passive `וְתֵרָאֶה הַיַּבָּשָׁה` (1:9). Mankind is "built" with no material and no purpose (20), Aruru builds the seed of mankind "with him" (21 ↔ 4:1 `קָנִיתִי אִישׁ אֶת יְהוָה`, our proposal); the order man → animals → rivers → plants is that of Gen 2, not Gen 1; the rivers are "named well" (24). Lines 34–40 return to 2–7 as Marduk's deeds: creation told twice, as absence and as act.
7. **"What shall we make?" (KAR 4).** After heaven was separated from earth, the gods ask *mīnâ i nīpuš, mīnâ i nibni* (16–20, `מה נעשה? מה נבנה?`): the only divine first-person-plural "let us make" before man in the corpus (↔ 1:26 `נַעֲשֶׂה אָדָם`), here a question answered by other gods. At Uzumua they slaughter the Illa-Alla gods and build mankind from their blood alone (24–26; = Enūma eliš VI, Atra-ḫasīs I); man's purpose is the gods' work quota (*iškāru* = `מִכְסָה`), the hoe and basket in his hands, the boundaries "for days of eternity" (27–41 ↔ 2:5, 2:15, 3:23). The first humans are named, Ullegarra and Annegarra (rev. 11–12 ↔ 5:2); cattle, fish and birds are listed with man but bred, not ruled (rev. 13–14 ↔ 1:26, 1:28); *dešû* D = `הִדְשִׁיא` (rev. ↔ 1:11). The Nineveh copy's catch-line is the first line of Atra-ḫasīs (our reading).

**Across the seven texts.** Man is made of clay (E&Nm), grows from the earth (Hoe; KAR 4 Sumerian), is built from slain gods' blood (KAR 4; EE VI), or is built with no material at all (Eridu; Adapa SB a+6): Genesis's `עָפָר מִן הָאֲדָמָה` and breath has a counterpart only for the material, never for the breath. Everywhere he is made for the gods' work; only Genesis gives the work to the ground for man's own bread (3:17–19). Naming the first humans (Hoe 29; KAR 4) ↔ 5:2. No Mesopotamian text here has a prohibition before the eating: the commandment of 2:16–17 is [absent] in both Adapa (a warning, misleading) and Enki and Ninḫursaĝa (no warning at all).

## Key findings for the commentary: projects 3–4 (Bird and Fish; Enki and the World Order) – Gen 1:14, 1:20–23, Gen 4, Deut 32:8

Written by the corpus coordinator from the two projects' indexes (2026-10-19); details and the line-by-line reasoning in `3-הציפור-והדג/project.md` and `4-אנקי-וסדר-העולם/project.md`. **B&F** = the Debate between Bird and Fish (ETCSL 5.3.5), **EWO** = Enki and the World Order (ETCSL 1.1.3).

1. **The fifth day's two creatures are made in one act, in one place (B&F 13–21).** Enki "settles fish and birds" in the pools (14) and "fills" (*si* = `מִלֵּא`) the reedbeds of the marsh with "fish and birds" (19), then gives them their "standing places" and shows them their "designs" (20–21: *ki-gub*, *ĝiš-ḫur*). This is the Sumerian counterpart of `יִשְׁרְצוּ הַמַּיִם … וְעוֹף יְעוֹפֵף` in one statement (1:20) and of `וּמִלְאוּ אֶת הַמַּיִם` (1:22) – but the god fills, the creatures do not swarm or multiply; there is no "kind" (`לְמִינֵהוּ` [absent]) and no blessing: a "good fate" is decreed (B&F 1; EWO's *nam tar* everywhere). Fish and birds are **neighbours** of one marsh, quarrelling over it, not two ends of an axis; nothing suggests that the bird "develops out of" the water-creatures (the commentary's reading of 1:20 has no support here, and no contradiction either: the text has no development at all).
2. **`עוֹף` as motion, `צִפּוֹר` as kind – the commentary's dictionary tested.** Sumerian names the bird by its kind (*mušen*, rendered `צִפּוֹר`) and its flight by a separate verb (*dal*, `עָף`). The closest line to 1:20 is the bird's boast **"I am Bird: in heaven flying-flying, on earth walking-walking"** (B&F 129: *dal-dal* reduplicated, as Polel `יְעוֹפֵף`; heaven and earth as its two domains, ↔ `עַל הָאָרֶץ עַל פְּנֵי רְקִיעַ הַשָּׁמָיִם`, with no firmament). The fish mocks the bird's flitting as pointless (47). In EWO 1–249 birds are never named by their flight.
3. **A Sumerian "zoology" of the two creatures (B&F's insults).** The fish is defined by what it lacks – no hips, arms, hands or feet, a mouth that goes round but cannot see behind (57–59) – and by its smell; it "slips" through the reeds under the bird's eyes (66; ↔ the dictionary's `רֶמֶשׂ`, "seen only in part"), lurks on the bottom "like a heavy fog" (82), and its spawn is heaped like sheaves (119; ↔ `שֶׁרֶץ`, the uncountable). The bird is defined by noise, droppings, greed and by its fate: the fowler's net, the fattening house, the daily offering (30–50). The *tanninim*-like monster here is **the bird**: in revenge she is "lion-faced, eagle-taloned", whirling like a storm (110–115; Anzu's iconography; ↔ the appendix's thunderbird against the underwater serpent, reversed in force: the bird calls herself the fish's demon and storm, 63, 65).
4. **Why the bird wins: the voice.** The verdict (168–177) gives the bird the Ekur and Enlil's table because "her cooing is good" (*dug₃*, the word kept for `טוֹב`): the only "good" of the debate is said of a sound close to gods and king, where Gen 1:21 says `כִּי טוֹב` of all the creatures together. Nearness to the divine is by voice and by service at the table, not by nature.
5. **The nest and the eggs** (B&F 22–23, 104–108, 149–153): the fish destroys the nest while the mother is out feeding her young; the bird spreads her limbs (*a₂-ur₂*, `אֵבֶר`) over the ruin (113) – reading aids for Deut 22:6–7 (the nest law) and Deut 32:11 (`יִפְרֹשׂ כְּנָפָיו … עַל אֶבְרָתוֹ`).
6. **Who fills the waters in EWO?** Enki fills the **Tigris** with his "water" – Sumerian *a* is water and semen (EWO 250–260); he *addresses* the marsh (*gu₃ de₂*, not naming) and *gives* it carp (274–275), as `וַיֹּאמֶר אֱלֹהִים יִשְׁרְצוּ הַמַּיִם` addresses the waters – but the fish are handed out, not brought forth. In all thirteen appointments nothing is blessed, multiplied or "according to its kind". The sea is a **terrifying holy shrine**, not a monster (285–298; [divergence] ↔ the *tanninim* and Tiāmat), entrusted to Nanše, who sends its fish and birds to Enlil (418–421). The only thing "filled" is Enlil's temple (the refrain 261, 299, 368).
7. **Gen 1:14 – days, months, years without luminaries** (EWO 17–19, 44–45): Enki "counts the days, brings the months into their houses, completes the years", and the completed years go to the divine assembly for decision. Genesis gives this office to the lights (`וְהָיוּ לְאֹתֹת וּלְמוֹעֲדִים וּלְיָמִים וְשָׁנִים`); EWO to the god himself, with no firmament and no separating. Utu (the sun) is appointed judge "in the circuit of heaven and earth" (380; ↔ `וְלִמְשֹׁל בַּיּוֹם`, 1:16–18; Ps 19:6–7), and is the son of the moon's wife: the moon first.
8. **Deut 32:8 – borders and allotment.** "Zones he heaped, boundary pegs he filled; for the Anuna – dwellings in the cities, plots in the fields" (EWO 370–373). The verbs differ from `בְּהַנְחֵל … בְּהַפְרִידוֹ … יַצֵּב גְּבֻלֹת`; the verb of `יַצֵּב` (*gub* "set standing") is the appointment formula itself (`אֶנְקִי לְצִדּוֹ הֶעֱמִיד`, 13×), whose object is a god, not a border; what is shared out is **places to gods**, not peoples to gods. EWO 1–249's journey of fates is a world map from the centre outwards (Sumer, Ur, Meluḫḫa, Magan, Dilmun, Elam and Marḫaši plundered, the Martu "who have no city" given animals) ↔ Gen 10 and Deut 32:8.
9. **Gen 4:17–22 – culture as divine office, not human invention.** The farmer Enkimdu and the shepherd Dumuzid are appointed one after the other (EWO 318–325, 358–367 ↔ Cain and Abel, 4:2; Jabal, 4:20); Kulla of the bricks and Mušdama the builder, whose vaults reach heaven "like the rainbow" (335–348 ↔ 11:3–4, 9:13); Ninmug the metalworker (406–411 ↔ Tubal-cain); Inana's drums of lament and joy (447–448 ↔ Jubal); weaving and Uttu (381–386). In Genesis every art is a human invention in Cain's line; in EWO every one is a god's or goddess's office.
10. **The one left without a portion** (EWO 387–450): Inana "alone is left without the rites" – the reverse of Levi, who has no land *because* the cult is his portion (Deut 10:9; Num 18:20); Enki's "what have I diminished for you? what shall I add?" (*lal* / *taḫ*) is the vocabulary of Num 27:4, 36:3 (our proposal). Her powers are the improper ones: "what is not to be destroyed you destroyed, what is not to be placed you placed" (446: *gul* / *sig₁₀*, **not "create"** – ETCSL's false echo for Deut 32:39 / Isa 45:7).
11. **Gen 11:4 as praise**: Sumer's and Ur's temples "touch heaven", "raise a hand to heaven", and Enlil "pronounced your name" (EWO 207–218): the tower and the name are gifts, not hubris.
12. **ETCSL's English again manufactures creation language**: EWO 361 "created" (*du₃* "built"), 446 "create" (*sig₁₀* "put"), 274/309/326 "called" (*gu₃ de₂* "addressed"); B&F's "like Enki" (166) is not in the Sumerian (B&F open decision 2).

**The ancient mind.** B&F is schoolboy entertainment: insults as a marsh "zoology", a bird humming a lullaby while the fish rages, glosses left by teachers; the verdict rewards the noise that the fish mocked. EWO orders the world through sex (the river as bride, clay "penetrated like oil", animals made to mate) and through administration (gods as canal inspectors, farmers of Enlil, tax-collectors, scribes); the map of creation is the economy of southern Iraq, and all abundance flows to Nippur. Its humour is Inana's complaint, answered by a father's catalogue of her uncontainable powers and a tease that she cannot even hang a rope in a well.

| בראשית א:ז | EE IV 138–140 | *mišlušša iškunam-ma šamāmī uṣṣallil; išdud maška maṣṣara ušaṣbit; mêša lā šūṣâ šunūti umtaʾʾir* | את מחציתה שם, וקרה (בה) את השמים; מתח את העור, נוצרים הציב; את מימיה לבל יוציאו – אותם צוה | `וַיַּעַשׂ אֱלֹהִים אֶת הָרָקִיעַ וַיַּבְדֵּל בֵּין הַמַּיִם אֲשֶׁר מִתַּחַת לָרָקִיעַ וּבֵין הַמַּיִם אֲשֶׁר מֵעַל לָרָקִיעַ` (בראשית א:ז); `וַיִּקְרָא אֱלֹהִים לָרָקִיעַ שָׁמָיִם` (בראשית א:ח); `הַמְקָרֶה בַמַּיִם עֲלִיּוֹתָיו` (תהלים קד:ג); `נוֹטֶה שָׁמַיִם כַּיְרִיעָה` (תהלים קד:ב); `וַיִּמְתָּחֵם כָּאֹהֶל לָשָׁבֶת` (ישעיהו מ:כב); `רֹקַע הָאָרֶץ` (ישעיהו מב:ה); `תַּרְקִיעַ עִמּוֹ לִשְׁחָקִים חֲזָקִים כִּרְאִי מוּצָק` (איוב לז:יח); `וַיְרַקְּעוּ אֶת פַּחֵי הַזָּהָב` (שמות לט:ג) | **[divergence]** (Gen 1:6–8) / [equivalent] (Ps 104:2–3; Isa 40:22) | **Akkadian has no *raqīʿu* and no cognate of רקע here.** Marduk's sky is (1) a *roof* (*ṣullulu*, "to put beams over") made of half of Tiāmat's corpse, (2) a *stretched skin* (*šadādu maška*; older reading *parka* "bolt", i.e. a barred door), (3) *guards* given an order. The function is shared with Gen 1:6–8: a barrier that keeps the upper waters from coming down. Hebrew רקע "to beat out (metal) into a thin sheet" (Ex 39:3; Num 17:3 `רִקֻּעֵי פַחִים`; Job 37:18 the sky "beaten out" "hard as a cast mirror"; Isa 42:5, Ps 136:6 of the earth) **adds** a solid, hammered surface made by a craftsman-god's word, and **removes** the corpse, the skin, the guards and the enemy: in Gen 1 the barrier needs no watchmen. The "stretching" of the heavens like a tent or curtain (נטה, מתח: Isa 40:22; Ps 104:2) is the biblical image closest to *šadādu maška*; Ps 104:3 `הַמְקָרֶה` is the closest to *ṣullulu*. The Hebrew translation never uses רָקִיעַ. |
| בראשית א:ז | EE IV 142 | *uštamḫir meḫret apsî šubat nudimmud* | השווה (אותו) לעומת האפסו, מושב נודימוד | `וַיַּבְדֵּל בֵּין הַמַּיִם אֲשֶׁר מִתַּחַת לָרָקִיעַ וּבֵין הַמַּיִם אֲשֶׁר מֵעַל לָרָקִיעַ` (בראשית א:ז); `גַּם אֶת זֶה לְעֻמַּת זֶה עָשָׂה הָאֱלֹהִים` (קהלת ז:יד) | [divergence] | An upper structure set as the counterpart of the lower waters (Apsû): the same two-storey cosmos as Gen 1:7. |

Rendered `הפריד`, never `הבדיל`. The same motif opens ETCSL 1.8.1.4 and 1.8.2.1. |
| בראשית א:ז | KAR 4 obv. 1 | Sum. *an ki-ta … bad-a-ta* | השמים מן הארץ הופרדו | `וַיַּבְדֵּל בֵּין הַמַּיִם אֲשֶׁר מִתַּחַת לָרָקִיעַ וּבֵין הַמַּיִם אֲשֶׁר מֵעַל לָרָקִיעַ` (בראשית א:ז) | [equivalent] | The separation of heaven and earth is a given at the opening (as in the Song of the Hoe), not a narrated act; never `הִבְדִּיל`. |
| בראשית א:ח | EE IV 141 | *šamê ībir ašrata iḫīṭ* | עבר את השמים, את הזבול בדק | `וַיִּקְרָא אֱלֹהִים לָרָקִיעַ שָׁמָיִם` (בראשית א:ח); `מִזְּבֻל קָדְשְׁךָ` (ישעיהו סג:טו) | [divergence] / false echo in eBL | *ašratu* is a poetic word for heaven; eBL's English "firmament" imports the Vulgate's word for רקיע. Restored *[mišil]ša*. |
| בראשית א:ט | EE VII 83 | *bānû erṣeti eliš mê mukīn elâti* | בונה הארץ ממעל למים, מכונן המרומים | `לְרֹקַע הָאָרֶץ עַל-הַמָּיִם` (תהלים קלו:ו); `כִּי-הוּא עַל-יַמִּים יְסָדָהּ` (תהלים כד:ב); `יִקָּווּ הַמַּיִם מִתַּחַת הַשָּׁמַיִם אֶל מָקוֹם אֶחָד וְתֵרָאֶה הַיַּבָּשָׁה` (בראשית א:ט) | **[equivalent]** / [divergence] | Earth built above the waters, heights established ↔ the third and second days; Ps 136:6 uses רקע (root of רקיע), with no Akkadian counterpart. |
| בראשית א:ט | Eridu obv. 10, 27 | *tâmtum-ma*; *mātāti appāri apum-ma* | ים (היו); הארצות – אגם וסוף (היו) | `יִקָּווּ הַמַּיִם מִתַּחַת הַשָּׁמַיִם אֶל מָקוֹם אֶחָד וְתֵרָאֶה הַיַּבָּשָׁה` (בראשית א:ט) | [divergence] | The lands pass from sea (10) to marsh and reed-bed (27): gradual emergence, no gathering of the waters, no command. | בראשית א:ט | Eridu obv. 18, 31, 32 | *eperī ibni … išpuk*; *tamlâ umalli*; *apa nābala iškun* | עפר בנה ושפך; מלוא מלא; סוף ויבשה שׂם | `וְתֵרָאֶה הַיַּבָּשָׁה` (בראשית א:ט) | [divergence] | **The dry land is made by labour** – dust built and poured beside the raft, a fill at the sea's edge, dry land "set" – where Gen 1:9 has the medial Nifal "let it appear" (the commentary's "development that happened by itself", `1010-…`). Our proposal. 32 rests partly on eBL's restoration. |

| בראשית א:כ | B&F 47 | *dal-dal-zu a-na-am₃* | ועופפך – מה הוא? | `וְעוֹף יְעוֹפֵף` (בראשית א:כ) | [equivalent] | *dal* "to fly" reduplicated = Polel `יְעוֹפֵף` (flitting back and forth). The kind is *mušen*, the motion *dal*: supports the commentary's `צִפּוֹר` / `עוֹף`. The fish mocks the flight as aimless. |
| בראשית א:כ | B&F 111 | *dal-le-bi saĝ im-gi₄* | במעופה – ראש השיבה | `וְעוֹף יְעוֹפֵף` (בראשית א:כ) | [equivalent] | The Akkadian gloss renders *dal* by *naprušu* "to fly" (*ipparaš*): for the OB scribes *dal* is a verb of motion. |

Man rises by himself towards the god above, not taken (`לֻקַּח`) from the ground by the god. *ki* = `הָאָרֶץ`: the adam/adamah pun is Hebrew only. ETCSL's "started to" is not in the Sumerian. |

### Atra-ḫasīs I (flood corpus): the creation rows

Cross-reference only; the full rows are in `../סיפורי-המבול-המסופוטמיים/1-אתרחסיס/project.md` (Tablet I) and the flood corpus's `parallels-index.md`.

| Atra-ḫasīs I | Akkadian | Hebrew rendering | Bible | Tag | Comment |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | *inūma ilū awīlum* | כשהאלים היו אדם | ב:ה `וְאָדָם אַיִן לַעֲבֹד`; א:כו | [equivalent] / [divergence] | Before man, the gods did man's work. *awīlum* singular-collective like `אָדָם`. |
| 2–6, 186 | *dullu*, *tupšikku*; *šupšik ilim awīlum lišši* | עמל, סל; את סל האל – האדם ישא | ב:ה, טו `לְעָבְדָהּ`; ג:יז–יט, כג | [divergence] | Labour is man's purpose in both; here it is the gods' burden transferred to him. *zabālu* = סבל [cognate]. |
| 25–26 | *Idiqlat*, *Purattu* | חִדֶּקֶל, פְּרָת | ב:יד | [cognate] / [divergence] | Dug by the gods vs. flowing from Eden. |
| 183, 189 | *libnī-ma lullâ awīlam*; *bāniat awīlūti* | תבנה קדמון – אדם; בונת האנושות | א:כו `נַעֲשֶׂה אָדָם`; ג:כ `אֵם כָּל חָי`; ב:כב `וַיִּבֶן` | [equivalent] / [divergence]; *banû* = בנה [cognate] | Creation decided in a divine council; purpose: labour vs. dominion and image. |
| 199, 207, 222, 230 | *ṭīdu/ṭiṭṭu*, *ruʾtu* | טיט, רוק | ב:ז `עָפָר מִן הָאֲדָמָה … וַיִּפַּח`; איוב לג:ו `מֵחֹמֶר קֹרַצְתִּי` | [cognate] (טיט) / [divergence] | Clay + god's flesh and blood + gods' spittle vs. dust + divine breath. |
| 202, 217 | *warḫum sebūtum u šapattum* | בראש החודש, בשביעי ובכסה | ב:ב–ג (seventh day) | [divergence]; *šapattu* ~ `שַׁבָּת` [cognate?] | Lunar purification days fixed at creation vs. the seventh day of rest. |
| 211–213, 219, 235 | *eṭemmu*; *ṭēmu* | אִט; טעם | ב:ז `נִשְׁמַת חַיִּים`; ישעיהו יט:ג `הָאִטִּים` | [cognate/loan] / [divergence] | The pun ṭēmu/eṭemmu kept as טעם/אט. |
| 212 | *ittu* | אות | ד:טו; ט:יב–יג | [cognate] / [divergence] | The sign is inside man vs. given externally. |
